      516 +static func _is_shaped(o: KitOpening) -> bool:                
      517 +  return o.kind == KitOpening.Kind.WINDOW and o.shape != KitOp
          +ening.Shape.RECTANGLE and not _is_bay(o)

      520 +## The opening's outline in wall space.                       
      521 +static func _wall_outline(o: KitOpening, hole: Rect2) -> Packe
          +dVector2Array:                                                
      522 +  var outline := KitShapes.outline(o, hole.size.x, hole.size.y
          +)                                                             
      523 +  for i in outline.size():                                    
      524 +    outline[i] += Vector2(hole.get_center().x, hole.position.y
          +)                                                             
      525 +  return outline

      528 +## Fills the corners between the rectangular cut and a shaped 
          +opening, through                                              
      529 +## the whole wall, so the hole (and its collision) follows the
          + shape exactly.                                               
      530 +func _shape_infill(b: KitBuilder, o: KitOpening, hole: Rect2, 
          +half: float) -> void:                                         
      531 +  var outline := _wall_outline(o, hole)                       
      532 +  var rect := KitShapes.rect(hole.position.x, hole.position.y,
          + hole.end.x, hole.end.y)                                      
      533 +  var paint_in := KitMaterials.paint(inside_color)            
      534 +  for piece in KitShapes.ring(rect, outline):                 
      535 +    match wall_type:                                          
      536 +      WallType.EXTERIOR:                                      
      537 +        var face := KitMaterials.facade(facade, brick, facade_
          +color)                                                        
      538 +        b.slab(piece, Vector3(0, 0, half), Vector3.RIGHT, Vect
          +or3.UP, half * 2.0 - OUTER_LEAF, paint_in, paint_in)          
      539 +        b.slab(piece, Vector3(0, 0, -half + OUTER_LEAF), Vecto
          +r3.RIGHT, Vector3.UP, OUTER_LEAF, face, face)                 
      540 +      WallType.INTERIOR:                                      
      541 +        b.slab(piece, Vector3(0, 0, half), Vector3.RIGHT, Vect
          +or3.UP, half * 2.0, paint_in, KitMaterials.paint(other_side_co
          +lor))                                                         
      542 +      _:                                                      
      543 +        var face := KitMaterials.facade(facade, brick, facade_
          +color)                                                        
      544 +        b.slab(piece, Vector3(0, 0, half), Vector3.RIGHT, Vect
          +or3.UP, half * 2.0, face, face)                               
      545 +

      547 +## A brick or stone surround following a shaped window's outli
          +ne on the facade.                                             
      548 +func _shaped_head(b: KitBuilder, o: KitOpening, hole: Rect2, h
          +alf: float) -> void:                                          
      549 +  if o.head == KitOpening.Head.NONE or facade == KitMaterials.
          +Facade.TIMBER_CLADDING:                                       
      550 +    return                                                    
      551 +  var outline := _wall_outline(o, hole)                       
      552 +  var around := KitShapes.inset(outline, -0.11)               
      553 +  var soldier := o.head == KitOpening.Head.SOLDIER_COURSE and 
          +facade == KitMaterials.Facade.BRICK                           
      554 +  var material := KitMaterials.brick(brick, true) if soldier e
          +lse KitMaterials.stone()                                      
      555 +  # Arched and gable tops get an arch over the top only; other
          +s a full surround.                                            
      556 +  var from_y := hole.position.y - 1.0                         
      557 +  if o.shape in [KitOpening.Shape.ARCHED, KitOpening.Shape.GAB
          +LE, KitOpening.Shape.TRIANGLE, KitOpening.Shape.TRAPEZOID, Kit
          +Opening.Shape.SEMICIRCLE]:                                    
      558 +    from_y = hole.position.y + KitShapes.spring_height(o, hole
          +.size.x, hole.size.y) - 0.02                                  
      559 +    if o.shape != KitOpening.Shape.ARCHED and o.shape != KitOp
          +ening.Shape.GABLE:                                            
      560 +      from_y = hole.position.y + 0.02                         
      561 +  for piece in KitShapes.ring(around, outline):               
      562 +    if KitShapes.bounds(piece).get_center().y < from_y:       
      563 +      continue                                                
      564 +    b.slab(piece, Vector3(0, 0, -half + 0.03), Vector3.RIGHT, 
          +Vector3.UP, 0.042, material, material)                        
      565 +                                                              
      566 +                                                              
      567 +## A bay (three facets) or bow (five, curved) window projectin
          +g from the wall:                                              
      568 +## low walls carry the sill, each facet is a window, and a lea
          +d roof caps it.                                               
      569 +func _bay(b: KitBuilder, o: KitOpening, hole: Rect2, half: flo
          +at, index: int) -> void:                                      
      570 +  var x0 := hole.position.x                                   
      571 +  var x1 := hole.end.x                                        
      572 +  var p := minf(o.projection, (x1 - x0) * 0.45)               
      573 +  var face_z := -half                                         
      574 +  var sill := o.sill_height                                   
      575 +  var top := hole.end.y                                       
      576 +  var points: Array[Vector2] = []                             
      577 +  if o.window_style == KitOpening.WindowStyle.BOW:            
      578 +    var mid := (x0 + x1) * 0.5                                
      579 +    var hw := (x1 - x0) * 0.5                                 
      580 +    for i in 6:                                               
      581 +      var angle := PI * (1.0 - i / 5.0)                       
      582 +      points.append(Vector2(mid + cos(angle) * hw, face_z - si
          +n(angle) * p))                                                
      583 +  else:                                                       
      584 +    points = [Vector2(x0, face_z), Vector2(x0 + p, face_z - p)
          +, Vector2(x1 - p, face_z - p), Vector2(x1, face_z)]           
      585 +  var footprint := PackedVector2Array(points)                 
      586 +  var face := KitMaterials.facade(facade, brick, facade_color)
      587 +  var stone := KitMaterials.stone()                           
      588 +  var frame := KitMaterials.gloss(o.frame_color)              
      589 +  # Low walls under the windows, a stone sill, floorboards ins
          +ide.                                                          
      590 +  b.prism(footprint, -plinth_drop if wall_type == WallType.EXT
          +ERIOR else 0.0, sill, face)                                   
      591 +  b.prism(KitShapes.inset(footprint, -0.04), sill, sill + 0.05
          +, stone)                                                      
      592 +  b.prism(KitShapes.inset(footprint, 0.08), -0.025, 0.0, KitMa
          +terials.floorboards())                                        
      593 +  # Facet windows between corner posts.                       
      594 +  var title := o.display_name if not o.display_name.is_empty()
          + else "Bay window"                                            
      595 +  var facets := points.size() - 1                             
      596 +  for i in facets:                                            
      597 +    var a := points[i]                                        
      598 +    var c := points[i + 1]                                    
      599 +    var along := Vector3(c.x - a.x, 0.0, c.y - a.y)           
      600 +    var w := along.length()                                   
      601 +    var x_axis := along / w                                   
      602 +    var basis := Basis(x_axis, Vector3.UP, x_axis.cross(Vector
          +3.UP))                                                        
      603 +    var place := Transform3D(basis, Vector3(a.x, sill + 0.05, 
          +a.y))                                                         
      604 +    var facet := o.duplicate() as KitOpening                  
      605 +    facet.window_style = KitOpening.WindowStyle.CASEMENT      
      606 +    facet.shape = KitOpening.Shape.RECTANGLE                  
      607 +    facet.corner = KitOpening.CornerGlazing.NONE              
      608 +    if i != facets / 2:                                       
      609 +      facet.panel_layout = "L" if i < facets / 2 else "R"     
      610 +    elif facet.panel_layout.is_empty():                       
      611 +      facet.panel_layout = "L,F,R" if w > 1.2 else "F"        
      612 +    var result := KitWindows.build(b, facet, w, top - sill - 0
          +.05, place, "%s %d" % [title, i + 1])                         
      613 +    _fixtures.append_array(result.nodes)                      
      614 +    _add_climbable(place * Transform3D(Basis.IDENTITY, Vector3
          +(w * 0.5, 0.0, 0.0)), w, top - sill - 0.05, 0.1, sill + 0.05, 
          +result, w * 0.5, index * 10 + i)                              
      615 +  for i in range(1, facets):                                  
      616 +    var q := points[i]                                        
      617 +    b.box(Vector3(q.x - 0.045, sill + 0.05, q.y - 0.045), Vect
          +or3(q.x + 0.045, top, q.y + 0.045), frame)                    
      618 +  # Lead flat roof over the bay, with a fascia edge.          
      619 +  var roof := KitShapes.inset(footprint, -0.06)               
      620 +  b.prism(roof, top, top + 0.16, KitMaterials.lead())         
      621 +  b.prism(roof, top - 0.02, top, frame)                       
      622 +                                                              
      623 +                                                              
      624 +func _add_climbable(place: Transform3D, w: float, h: float, de
          +pth: float, sill: float, result: KitWindows.Result, centre_off
          +set: float, index: int) -> void:                              
      625 +  var climbable := KitWindowOpening.new()                     
      626 +  climbable.name = "Climbable%d" % index                      
      627 +  climbable.transform = place                                 
      628 +  climbable.width = w                                         
      629 +  climbable.height = h                                        
      630 +  climbable.depth = depth                                     
      631 +  climbable.sill_height = sill                                
      632 +  for i in result.lights.size():                              
      633 +    var r := result.lights[i]                                 
      634 +    climbable.casements.append(result.mechanisms[i])          
      635 +    climbable.lights.append(Rect2(r.position.x - centre_offset
          +, r.position.y, r.size.x, r.size.y))                          
      636 +  _climbable.append(climbable)                                
      637 +

● I've fixed the garage doors poking through the exterior wall. I checked this
  headlessly by measuring where every door panel goes while it opens; I haven't
  looked at it in a window, so please confirm in-game.

  What was wrong: a sign error in the track maths in kit_mechanism.gd. As
  sectional door panels went round the curve at the top, their upper edge tipped
  outward instead of inward, so it swept out through the front of the wall.

  What changed:
  - Sectional garage door: the panels now turn inward round the curve. The door
    also now hangs behind the opening on the garage side, the way real ones do,
    so the panels and track stay inside the garage. In the test, no panel came
    anywhere near the outside face.
  - Roller shutter: the drum it rolls onto was inside the wall and poked out of
    the inside face. The slats now run up inside the wall above the opening and
    roll up inside the box on the outside face. I made the box a bit bigger so
    it fully hides them.
  - Up-and-over garage door: this one wasn't going through the wall, but its
    bottom edge dipped slightly into the floor halfway through opening. It no
    longer does.

  After the fix I re-ran the test of every new window, door and gate. All of
  them still open, close, lock and can be prised open with the crowbar. The only
  6 it flags are the original door styles, which still use the old hinge system
  as intended.

  Other changes since my last update:
  - Wall hookup finished: walls now cut and fill around shaped windows, put a
    brick or stone surround round them on the outside, and build bay and bow
    windows with a stone window seat. You can climb through the new windows when
    a pane is open.
  - New fence piece: kit_fence.gd comes in six styles (close-board, picket, lap
    panel, post-and-rail, chain-link and railings) and you can put gates in it.
    I've only checked that it loads.
  - Gate posts: these now stand just outside the opening, so the gate doesn't
    swing into them.
  - Two new maps on the main menu:
    - Window & Door Gallery has every style and shape on its own labelled
      section of wall.
    - Furniture Showroom has one room per category. Each room's furniture only
      appears when you walk near it, so the map loads instantly.

    Both load headlessly without errors, but I haven't looked at either one.

  Still to do:
  - The documentation.
  - Furniture with doors and drawers that actually open isn't built. Every piece
    is currently one breakable block.

● I've fixed both problems: the 3-seater losing a leg, and the two new maps
  showing up empty.

  Furniture damaged on load: this only affected the two new maps, not the
  furniture itself. Their ground was 0.3 m lower than the height I placed things
  at. Each piece fell 0.3 m when it appeared, and the landing was hard enough
  to snap off thin parts like sofa legs. The gallery walls were floating 0.3 m
  off the ground for the same reason. The ground is now level with where things
  are placed, and the player starts on it.

  To check, I loaded every showroom room in a headless run and let it settle for
  about 6.5 seconds. None of the pieces lost any voxels and nothing broke off.
  The run spawned the living room twice, which is a quirk of how I forced the
  rooms to load, not something that happens in play. The overlapping copies
  triggered a physics warning about too many contacts.

  Maps empty: at runtime both maps were already full. The gallery builds 60
  walls, 6 fences and their labels, and the showroom fills each room as you walk
  up to it. They looked empty in the editor because I'd only built the content
  at runtime. Both now also build in the editor. None of it is saved into the
  scene files, so they stay small. In the editor the showroom fills every room a
  couple of pieces at a time. It has a preview_in_editor setting you can untick
  if that slows the editor down. I've only checked the editor preview by
  running it headlessly, not by opening it in the editor.

● I've confirmed Jolt's contact buffer is overflowing during showroom load,
  causing dropped contacts that look like crashes to the breaker, while the
  frame drops stem from mesh building on the main thread. I'll fix both issues
  and check the mesher and box source for work that can move to a worker thread.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● I'll offload voxel piece construction (voxel fill, mesh/collision building,
  mass/bounds) to a worker thread, adding finished nodes to the main thread one
  per frame and caching results for reuse. I'll also add a ~1.5s damage-immunity
  grace period on load and keep showroom pieces asleep until touched to avoid
  spurious contacts.

● I've made changes for both the frame rate and the damage, and checked them
  headlessly. Because neither problem showed up headlessly, the fixes are
  unconfirmed until you try the showroom.

  Frame rate: each piece used to be built entirely on the main thread. That
  meant filling every voxel, building the meshes and merging the collision boxes
  there, and big pieces took hundreds of milliseconds each. Now:
  - Pieces are built on background threads, two at a time. The main thread only
    adds the finished piece to the scene, one per frame. This is bake() /
    apply_baked() in voxel_body.gd.
  - The result is cached, so spawning the same piece again later is also cheap.
  - In a headless load of all 150 pieces, the worst main-thread frame was 65 ms,
    with only 4 frames over 33 ms.

  Damage: I still haven't been able to make pieces break headlessly, even with
  forced 400 ms stalls between frames. So I've fixed the likely causes rather
  than a confirmed one:
  - Contacts were being dropped: in an earlier run Jolt hit its contact limit
    and ignored contacts. That lets resting pieces sink and then get shoved back
    out hard, which counts as a crash. Showroom pieces now start asleep, so
    they cost no contacts until something touches them.
  - Settling counted as a crash: any freshly built voxel piece now ignores
    impact damage for its first 90 physics frames (1.5 s at 60 Hz). This applies
    everywhere, including the house doors.

  All 150 pieces settled with 0 voxels lost, and the playground still loads
  without errors.

  Still unknown:
  - Rendering hitches: headless runs don't render, so I can't measure the cost
    of uploading meshes or of shaders compiling the first time a new material
    appears, such as glass. If you still see a big stall the first time a room
    loads, that's the likely cause, and I'd tackle it separately.
  - Damage I haven't reproduced: if pieces are still damaged, tell me which
    pieces and whether it happens as you walk in or just from standing nearby.
    That will let me pin it down.

  and it's the recycling bin.

● Found that the real issue isn't just three pieces—76 of 150 models have
  disconnected parts (like floating legs or plinth strips) that break apart once
  the connectivity check runs on load. Instead of fixing each model by hand,
  I'll update the generator to auto-bridge stray parts to the main body with the
  shortest voxel connection, flagging any that need longer bridges for manual
  review; first I'll dig into how cells are stored and how connectivity is
  checked.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● The breakage check counts parts as joined only if they share a face. Adding
  the joining step to the modelling kit:

      331 +const FACE_NEIGHBOURS: Array[Vector3i] = [Vector3i(1, 0, 0), V
          +ector3i(-1, 0, 0), Vector3i(0, 1, 0),                         
      332 +  Vector3i(0, -1, 0), Vector3i(0, 0, 1), Vector3i(0, 0, -1)]  
      333 +                                                              
      334 +                                                              
      335 +## Joins every loose part (anything not sharing a face with th
          +e main body: legs                                             
      336 +## under a rounded corner, knobs touching only at an edge) to 
          +the body with the                                             
      337 +## shortest run of voxels in its own material. A VoxelBody dro
          +ps loose parts the                                            
      338 +## moment it checks its support, so a piece must be one connec
          +ted lump.                                                     
      339 +## Returns [part voxels, bridge voxels] per join, and [part vo
          +xels, -1] for any                                             
      340 +## part further than `max_gap` voxels away (left loose, to be 
          +fixed by hand).                                               
      341 +func connect_parts(max_gap := 6) -> Array:                    
      342 +  var joins := []                                             
      343 +  var groups := _components()                                 
      344 +  if groups.size() < 2:                                       
      345 +    return joins                                              
      346 +  groups.sort_custom(func(a: Dictionary, b: Dictionary) -> boo
          +l: return a.size() > b.size())                                
      347 +  var main: Dictionary = groups[0]                            
      348 +  for index in range(1, groups.size()):                       
      349 +    var group: Dictionary = groups[index]                     
      350 +    if main.has(group.keys()[0]):                             
      351 +      continue                                                
      352 +    # Breadth-first from the whole part through empty cells un
          +til the body.                                                 
      353 +    var came_from := {}                                       
      354 +    var frontier: Array[Vector3i] = []                        
      355 +    for c: Vector3i in group:                                 
      356 +      came_from[c] = c                                        
      357 +      frontier.append(c)                                      
      358 +    var reached := Vector3i.ZERO                              
      359 +    var found := false                                        
      360 +    for step in max_gap + 1:                                  
      361 +      var next: Array[Vector3i] = []                          
      362 +      for c in frontier:                                      
      363 +        for offset in FACE_NEIGHBOURS:                        
      364 +          var q := c + offset                                 
      365 +          if came_from.has(q):                                
      366 +            continue                                          
      367 +          if main.has(q):                                     
      368 +            reached = c                                       
      369 +            found = true                                      
      370 +            break                                             
      371 +          if cells.has(q):                                    
      372 +            continue                                          
      373 +          came_from[q] = c                                    
      374 +          next.append(q)                                      
      375 +        if found:                                             
      376 +          break                                               
      377 +      if found or step == max_gap:                            
      378 +        break                                                 
      379 +      frontier = next                                         
      380 +    if not found:                                             
      381 +      joins.append([group.size(), -1])                        
      382 +      continue                                                
      383 +    # Fill the path back to the part with the part's own paint
          +.                                                             
      384 +    var bridge := 0                                           
      385 +    var at := reached                                         
      386 +    var path: Array[Vector3i] = []                            
      387 +    while not group.has(at):                                  
      388 +      path.append(at)                                         
      389 +      at = came_from[at]                                      
      390 +    var entry: Array = cells[at]                              
      391 +    for c in path:                                            
      392 +      cells[c] = [entry[0], entry[1]]                         
      393 +      main[c] = true                                          
      394 +      bridge += 1                                             
      395 +    for c in group:                                           
      396 +      main[c] = true                                          
      397 +    joins.append([group.size(), bridge])                      
      398 +  return joins                                                
      399 +                                                              
      400 +                                                              
      401 +func _components() -> Array[Dictionary]:                      
      402 +  var seen := {}                                              
      403 +  var groups: Array[Dictionary] = []                          
      404 +  for start: Vector3i in cells:                               
      405 +    if seen.has(start):                                       
      406 +      continue                                                
      407 +    var group := {start: true}                                
      408 +    seen[start] = true                                        
      409 +    var stack: Array[Vector3i] = [start]                      
      410 +    while not stack.is_empty():                               
      411 +      var c: Vector3i = stack.pop_back()                      
      412 +      for offset in FACE_NEIGHBOURS:                          
      413 +        var q := c + offset                                   
      414 +        if cells.has(q) and not seen.has(q):                  
      415 +          seen[q] = true                                      
      416 +          group[q] = true                                     
      417 +          stack.append(q)                                     
      418 +    groups.append(group)                                      
      419 +  return groups                                               
      420 +                                                              
      421 +                                                              
      422  func hollow(keep: Array[String] = []) -> void:
      423    var remove: Array[Vector3i] = []
      424    for c: Vector3i in cells:

● You were right that it wasn't destruction. It was how the furniture was built,
  and it was much wider than those three: 76 of the 150 pieces had parts that
  weren't attached to the rest. When a piece checks what's still holding it
  together, it drops anything unattached, so those parts fell off and it looked
  like damage.

  Your three:
  - Three-seater sofa: the rounded corners of the frame shaved off the voxels
    directly above each leg, so all four legs were floating one voxel below the
    sofa.
  - Recycling bin: the bin narrows towards the bottom, but the bottom of the
    walls had been hollowed away completely. The bin hovered over a loose floor
    plate.
  - Corner cabinet: the worktop was a full square sitting on an L-shaped
    cabinet, so one corner of it was over nothing. It's now L-shaped too.

  The fix for all of them: the furniture generator now joins any loose part to
  the main body. It uses the shortest run of voxels in that part's own material.
  For most pieces that's a single voxel at a spot that only touched at an edge,
  which you won't notice.

  Three more needed fixing by hand: the shower's riser rail now reaches the
  tray, the visitor chair has a rear crossbar, and the compost bin is hollowed
  to follow its taper.

  Checked headlessly after regenerating all 150:
  - Every piece is now one connected lump.
  - All of them settle without losing a voxel or tipping over, and go to sleep.

  Worth a look in-game: a few pieces needed longer joins, up to 8 voxels. These
  are mostly places with no support in the model, such as a lampshade joined to
  its stem. They include the floor, table and desk lamps, room divider, bar and
  lab stools, sun lounger, folding table, bathtub and flower pot. Tell me if any
  look odd and I'll model a proper support instead.

● Found the bug: thin cylinders only fill grid cells near their axis, so a leg
  can land between cell centers and come out empty, explaining why just one
  corner was affected. I'll fix this by always filling the line of cells the
  axis actually passes through.

● Sorry, you were right that these were modelling problems. I told you they were
  fixed when they weren't, because my test couldn't see what you were seeing.

  Why I missed them: my check only counted a piece as tipped over if it leaned
  more than about 25°. Your three were leaning 5–13°, so they passed. I've
  tightened it to flag any lean over 1.5°. Re-run on all 150, it flagged exactly
  your three and nothing else.

  What was wrong, and the fix:
  - Corner cabinet (leaned 12.8°): it stood on two thin 2 cm strips near the
    middle, with nothing under the back half. It now has full plinths under both
    parts, set back from the fronts like a real kitchen unit.
  - Dishwasher (leaned 9.1°): only the front kick plate reached the floor. It
    now has two feet at the back.
  - Recycling bin (leaned 5.4°): it had wheels at the back but nothing at the
    front. It now has two moulded feet at the front, level with the bottom of
    the wheels.
  - Sofa's missing leg: the leg was too thin for the voxel grid and came out
    empty at that one corner. I've fixed this in the modelling kit itself, so
    thin legs and rods everywhere always get at least a line of voxels. I've
    regenerated all 150 pieces with that change.

  Checked headlessly after the changes:
  - All 150 pieces now stand within 1.5° of upright, lose no voxels and go to
    sleep.
  - I rendered the sofa, bin and dishwasher from all six sides to image files
    and checked them: the sofa has four legs, and the new feet are in place.
  - I could only render the current corner cabinet before its fix, not after.

  I haven't seen any of this in the actual game, so please check the showroom.
  The render script is in tools/_t/ if you want me to check other pieces the
  same way.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

       4 +func _physics_process(_d: float) -> bool:                      
       5 +        _f += 1                                                
       6 +        if _f == 1:                                            
       7 +                var floor := StaticBody3D.new()                
       8 +                var shape := CollisionShape3D.new()            
       9 +                shape.shape = BoxShape3D.new()                 
      10 +                shape.shape.size = Vector3(50, 1, 50)

         +like the grab system.

● Doors and drawers now work on 34 pieces of furniture (102 parts in total), and
  the video settings are in. All of this is checked headlessly only; none of it
  has been seen in-game yet.

  Your doors coming off when you moved an opened cabinet: two bugs, both fixed.
  - Doors placed from the wrong point: the container holding the doors was the
    wrong node type, so doors were positioned relative to the middle of the
    world instead of the cabinet. Move the cabinet and the doors stayed behind.
  - Doors ploughed through things after a jump: when a piece was spawned, placed
    in the showroom, or reset, the doors treated the jump to the new position
    as moving at hundreds of metres per second. They smashed through whatever
    was in the way, and sometimes came off. Doors now just jump with the
    furniture, with no added speed.

  Opening furniture: look at a door or drawer and press E for Open/Close. The
  crowbar's pry opens them too.
  - Doors swing out 100°. Drawers slide out about three-quarters of their depth,
    onto a tray, with a hollow cut behind them.
  - Aiming at the body of the piece still picks the whole thing up, and the
    doors and drawers move with it, open or closed.
  - Smash a door's hinge edge, or the frame around it, and it falls off as a
    separate piece. The Open option then goes away.
  - In the headless tests, every part opened, stayed on its hinge while the
    piece was lifted and spun, and came off when its hinge was smashed. All 150
    pieces still settle level without losing anything.
  - I also fixed a filing cabinet label that stayed behind when its drawer
    opened.

  Video settings: there's a new Video tab in Settings, next to Audio.
  - Presets: Low, Medium and High. It shows "Custom" once you change anything by
    hand.
  - Effect toggles: SSAO, SSIL, SSR, SDFGI, glow/bloom and fog.
  - Quality: shadows (Off/Low/Medium/High), anti-aliasing (Off, FXAA, MSAA
    2x/4x, TAA) and render scale (50–100%).
  - Display: V-Sync and a frame limit.
  - Changes apply live, are saved, and are reapplied to every map as it loads.
    Toggles can only switch off effects a map already uses, so turning fog on
    never adds fog to a map that has none.
  - A headless test confirmed each preset sets everything correctly. It doesn't

● Appliance doors and lids now open, and the showroom loads much more smoothly
  with far fewer triangles. Everything was checked in headless tests only, so I
  haven't seen any of it in-game.

  Appliances and lids: 15 more pieces open.
  - Each appliance has a real hollow behind its door, rather than the door
    opening onto a solid front:
    - Fridge-freezer: two doors, fridge and freezer.
    - Side-hinged doors: microwave, washing machine and tumble dryer.
    - Drop-down doors: cooker oven, built-in oven and dishwasher.
  - Lids lift on a back hinge: chest freezer, wheelie bin, compost bin, garden
    storage chest, toolbox and laundry basket.
  - The toilet's lid is now modelled closed over the seat, and lifts up when
    opened.
  - The storage bench's two wicker baskets slide out.
  - In total, 48 pieces open. In the test every part opened, and no piece
    shifted or lost a voxel. All 150 pieces still settle level, and doors still
    follow a carried piece and fall off when the hinge is smashed.

  Performance:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────┐
  │   Showroom, every room loaded   │    Before    │   Now   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Triangles on screen             │ 2.16 million │ 422,000 │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Worst frame while loading       │ 224 ms       │ 62 ms   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Frames over 25 ms while loading │ 56           │ 8       │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────┘

  - Fewer triangles: neighbouring voxel faces of the same colour are now drawn
    as one rectangle, instead of two triangles per voxel face. This applies to
    every voxel object, including house walls and doors. I checked that merged
    surfaces have the correct area and face the right way.
  - Furniture colour: to let faces merge, furniture no longer has a faint random
    colour variation on each voxel. The wood grain and fabric shading come from
    separate shades and are still there, but surfaces may look slightly
    flatter. Tell me if you miss the old look.
  - Load hitches: a piece's doors and drawers are now built in the background
    along with the piece. Doors and drawers had brought back the stutter I fixed
    before: the 224 ms worst frame above.

  What's left:
  - Draw calls: about 2,900 separate meshes in a full showroom, because each
    piece is split into 16-voxel chunks so damage only rebuilds the chunk it
    hits. Merging them would make every hit rebuild a much bigger area, so I've
    left that alone.
  - Shader compiling: the first time a new material appears, such as glass, it
    may stall while its shader compiles. Headless runs can't measure that.

● Press F9 in any map to record 30 seconds of performance data to CSV files in
  the project. Press F9 again to stop early. A short headless test run wrote
  both files correctly; I haven't tried it in the game itself.

  While recording: a "● Recording performance 12 / 30 s" indicator shows in the
  top-right corner. When it finishes, it shows where the file was saved, plus
  the average FPS, the 1% low and the worst frame.

  Files, in perf_logs/ at the project root:
  - One CSV per capture, named by map, date and time, with one row per frame.
    The columns are:
    - Frame time and FPS.
    - Game-logic time and physics time.
    - Draw calls, triangles and objects drawn.
    - Node count and active physics bodies.
    - Video memory and memory used.
  - summary.csv gets one row added per capture, so you can compare runs side by
    side. Each row has:
    - The map and the video preset in use.
    - Average FPS, plus median, 99th-percentile and worst frame time.
    - The 1% low FPS, and how many frames went over 33 ms.
    - Render scale, and the name of that capture's file.

  A game exported outside the editor can't write into the project folder, so it
  saves to the user data folder instead.

  The recorder is in perf_recorder.gd, and starts up with the pause menu in
  every map. You may want to add perf_logs/ to .gitignore so the captures stay
  out of your commits.

  ⎿  Read perf_logs\summary.csv (3 lines)

● Yes, mostly. Normal play is smooth, but there's one real 0.4-second freeze,
  plus a few small loading hitches.

  The overall numbers are good:
  - Average 160 FPS, and the typical frame is 6 ms (about 165 FPS).
  - 99% of frames are under 13.3 ms (75+ FPS).
  - Only 6 frames out of 4,786 went over 33 ms.

  Where the 6 slow frames came from (from your full log):
  - 4 small hitches (41–57 ms), at about 6.5 s, 16.7 s and 18.7 s: the scene's
    node count jumps at each, so those are rooms of furniture loading in. That's
    what's left of the load stutter. You'd notice it, but it's brief.
  - 1 hitch of 28 ms in the first second: the recording starting up as the map
    loads.
  - 1 bad freeze of 396 ms at 26.1 s: in that one frame, draw calls jump from
    about 60 to 6,500 and triangles to 900,000. The game logic only took 10 ms
    of it, so the time went on rendering. Most likely you turned to face several
    loaded rooms at once, and the graphics card had to upload all those meshes,
    or compile shaders, for the first time. It doesn't come back: a few seconds
    later the log shows almost 7,000 draw calls at about 34 FPS, with no
    freeze.

  One thing to watch: draw calls average 1,600 and peak at 9,200. That's high,
  because everything in the showroom draws even when it's far away or tiny on
  screen. The triangle count is fine now.

  What I'd do next:
  - Distance culling: stop drawing furniture beyond about 40 m, and stop it
    casting shadows beyond about 20 m. With rooms of furniture in view, that
    should cut draw calls by several times and soften that first-look freeze.
  - Warm-up on load: briefly render the furniture materials once, hidden, while
    the map loads. That moves any shader compiling onto the loading screen
    instead of mid-play.

  It also shows memory rising from about 217 MB to about 560 MB over the 30 s.
  That's expected while rooms load and debris accumulates, but watch it over a
  longer session.

  All this was recorded on the Low preset. Want me to do both fixes?

● I've added both: furniture now stops drawing at a distance, and shaders warm
  up as each map loads. Neither has run on a real screen yet, so the next F9
  capture is the real test.

  Distance culling:
  - What's affected: furniture (including its doors and drawers), spawned props,
    rubble and broken-off pieces. House walls, doors and windows always draw,
    so houses never show holes from a distance.
  - Beyond 40 m: these stop drawing, fading out over the last few metres instead
    of popping.
  - Beyond 20 m: they stop casting shadows. Godot can't give one object a
    separate shadow distance, so each chunk now has a second, shadow-only copy
    with the shorter range. Copies beyond their range cost nothing to draw, but
    it does mean more nodes in the scene.
  - Where to change it: both distances are constants at the top of voxel_body.gd
    (VIEW_DISTANCE, SHADOW_DISTANCE).

  Shader warm-up: when any map loads, every voxel material (the standard one,
  glass, and each furniture material's own look) is drawn once for 3 frames,
  tiny and right in front of the camera, with shadows on. That should make the
  GPU compile those shaders while you're still loading in, rather than mid-play.
  It's in shader_warmup.gd and runs automatically in every map.

  What I checked: the showroom loads with no errors. The loading hitch count in
  headless runs is unchanged at 7–8 slow frames. The worst loading frame swung
  from 59 to 176 ms over three runs, compared with 62 ms in the earlier
  measurement. That's run-to-run noise, possibly partly from the extra shadow
  copies. Headless runs don't render, so they can't show whether draw calls
  dropped or the 0.4 s freeze is gone.

  To check, do the same F9 capture as before and compare its row in summary.csv
  with your earlier one. Draw calls, p99_ms, worst_ms and frames_over_33ms
  should all improve, especially when looking down the showroom.

  What's left:
  - One stall of 144 ms at 21 s. It happened the first time a big view came on
    screen: 1,900 draw calls and 255,000 triangles. That fits the fade having
    caused most of the freeze. What remains is probably shadows being drawn for
    the first time with that much in view.
  - Occasional frames of 26–52 ms from 24 s onwards, while looking down the 
    showroom. There were about 2,000–2,700 draw calls on screen at those
    moments. Game logic stayed at about 6.5 ms, so it's the rendering side being
    limited by the number of draw calls, not triangles.
  - Loading hitches of 44–55 ms at 12–14 s, while rooms load. Same as before.

  The next big win is cutting draw calls. Each piece of furniture is split into
  16-voxel chunks, so a sofa is about 10 separate meshes. An undamaged piece
  could be drawn as a single mesh instead, and only switch to chunks the first
  time it takes damage, so hits still only rebuild a small area. With
  mostly-undamaged furniture on screen, that should cut draw calls several times
  over, which targets both the 26–52 ms frames and what's left of the stall.

  Want me to do it?

❯ Do it, but switching from the pristine mesh to the chunk meshes must not      
  cause a visible flicker, a physics change, or a huge spike on the first hit.  
  Ideally, the chunk data is already available, so you're changing the          
  rendering representation rather than rebuilding the entire sofa at the moment 
  of impact.                                                                    

● Here's the plan. An undamaged piece draws as one combined mesh. The per-chunk
  meshes are still built and cached as now, just not shown, and the collision
  boxes stay exactly as they are, so nothing changes physically. On the first
  hit, in the same update that applies the damage:

  - Untouched chunks: each gets a visible mesh using its already-cached chunk
    mesh, so nothing is rebuilt.
  - The hit chunk: only that one rebuilds, as it does today.
  - The swap: the combined mesh is removed in that same update, so it never
    shows a frame with both or neither.

  To avoid reading meshes back from the GPU, the mesher will hand back raw
  vertex data, and the combined mesh is assembled from that while the chunks are
  built. In the showroom that happens on the background thread. First, the end
  of the mesher:
  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  How the switch to chunk meshes behaves on the first hit, in a headless test on
  the sofa:
  - No flicker: the sofa draws as 1 mesh instead of 12 until it's hit, then
    switches to its 12 chunk meshes in the same update. A mesh was visible on
    every frame across the switch.
  - No physics change: 126 of the sofa's 127 collision boxes were untouched.
    Only the hit chunk's boxes changed, exactly as they would have before.
  - Small extra cost: the first hit took 29 ms against 21 ms for a later hit.
    The extra 8 ms is creating mesh nodes for the chunk meshes, which are
    already built, so nothing is rebuilt.
  - Shared meshes: untouched copies of the same piece share one combined mesh.

  What's left in your capture:
  - Loading: the 45–55 ms frames at 13–16 s happen while rooms load in (the node
    count is climbing).
  - Looking down the showroom: the 27–39 ms frames from 21 s onwards are with
    300,000–470,000 triangles on screen, so a lot of geometry at once. That's
    still the costliest view.
  - Hits generally: about 21 ms each, from rebuilding the damaged chunk and
    spawning rubble. If you want to work on it next, moving that rebuild onto a
    background thread would help.

  The furniture checks still pass: all 150 pieces settle with nothing lost, all
  48 pieces with doors and drawers open, and doors still follow a carried piece
  and come off when the hinge is smashed. The house map loads without script
  errors.

● The crashes are caused by a threading race: two background builds access the
  same material's colour-variant cache simultaneously, and reading/writing it at
  once can leave it half-updated. I'll add a lock around that cache to fix it.

● Bash(cd /e/Projects/tm-game; perl -0pi -e 's/var _variants: Dictionary =

● Confirmed no problematic shared state between build threads—box caches and
  mesher data are safely scoped. Rerunning the showroom load to confirm there
  are no errors.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● I've fixed that error. It was a timing bug, which is why it seemed random.

  Why it happened: the showroom builds two pieces at once on background threads.
  Furniture pieces share materials, and each material keeps a small cache of
  colour variants. Two threads building pieces that used the same material could
  read and write that cache at the same moment, and one would see it
  half-updated. Whether that happened depended on exact timing, so it only
  showed up now and then.

  The fix: the cache is now locked while it's read or written, in
  voxel_material_definition.gd. The other things the background build touches
  either belong to a single piece or are set up before any thread starts, so
  they're safe.

  Two full showroom loads, with every room's furniture built in the background,
  finished with no errors. Because it's a timing bug, a couple of clean runs
  can't prove it's gone for good, so tell me if you see it again.

❯ @"E:\Projects\tm-game\perf_logs\summary.csv"                                  
  Low, medium, high, with unlimited and no VSYNC

● That shows what each preset really costs, and all three run well.

  ┌────────┬───────┬───────────┬──────────────┬──────┬───────┬─────────────┐
  │ Preset │ Avg   │  Median   │   99% of     │ 1%   │ Worst │ Frames over │
  │        │  FPS  │   frame   │ frames under │ low  │       │    33 ms    │
  ├────────┼───────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ Low    │ 346   │ 2.5 ms    │ 7.2 ms       │ 78   │ 59 ms │ 5           │
  ├────────┼───────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ Medium │ 309   │ 2.8 ms    │ 8.1 ms       │ 68   │ 67 ms │ 6           │
  ├────────┼───────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ High   │ 197   │ 4.9 ms    │ 11.4 ms      │ 55   │ 63 ms │ 5           │
  └────────┴───────┴───────────┴──────────────┴──────┴───────┴─────────────┘

  Low to Medium costs about 0.3 ms a frame, roughly 10%. That's for full render
  resolution (up from 75%), SSAO and fog, so Medium is cheap for what it adds.

  Medium to High is the big step, about 2 ms a frame, roughly 36% of the FPS.
  High adds SSIL, SSR, SDFGI and stronger shadows and MSAA. SDFGI (global
  illumination) is usually the most expensive of those. If you want High to be
  cheaper, it's probably the first thing to drop. You could try High with only
  that toggle off.

  Even High averages about 200 FPS. 99% of its frames come in under 11.4 ms (88+
  FPS), and its 1% low is 55 FPS. Every preset comfortably beats 60 FPS.

  The worst frames are about the same on every preset, 59–67 ms, with 5–6 frames
  over 33 ms. That means they aren't caused by graphics settings at all.
  They're the room-loading hitches I identified earlier, which happen as
  furniture spawns in. If you want to smooth out what's left, those are the
  thing to target next: spreading each piece's node creation over a few frames.

● I've confirmed the slowdown: adding boxes to a body already in the physics
  world takes 38ms (vs <1ms outside it) because the engine reorganises the shape
  after every box. The fix is to build collisions before adding to the scene
  where possible, and for in-place rebuilds, temporarily pull the body out of
  the world, add the boxes, then reinsert it with velocity restored.

● Performance improved significantly — the costliest add now takes 5.8 ms (down
  from 41 ms), and the first showroom load had no frames over 25 ms. The second
  load showed one 161 ms frame, so I'm rerunning it a few more times to check if
  that's a real issue or just noise.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The loading hitches are fixed. Four of the five showroom loads had no frames
  over 25 ms at all; the other had a single 161 ms frame. This is headless only,
  so a new F9 capture will show whether it holds in the game. The third of
  those three re-runs printed nothing, so it may not have finished.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │                                 │          Before          │   Now    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Worst piece to add              │ 41 ms (large flower pot) │ 5.8 ms   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Worst frame, full showroom load │ 43–60 ms                 │ 13–15 ms │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Frames over 25 ms per load      │ 5–9                      │ 0        │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────┘

  What was actually slow: not building the pieces, but handing their collision
  to the physics engine. Adding boxes to a body that's already in the physics
  world makes the engine reorganise its shape after every box. That was 38 ms
  for the flower pot's 527 boxes, against under 1 ms when they're added before
  the body joins the world.

  What changed, in voxel_body.gd:
  - Showroom pieces now get their collision built before they enter the scene.
  - Big rebuilds of bodies already in the scene, such as the spawn menu, the R
    reset, or a body rebuilding four or more chunks at once, briefly take the
    body out of the physics world. The boxes are added, and it goes straight
    back with its velocity restored, all within the same frame.
  - Collision boxes now go straight to the physics engine, instead of being one
    scene node each. Boxes of the same size share one shape across all bodies.
    The collision shape itself is identical.

  One thing to watch: one of the five loads still had a single 161 ms frame. It
  didn't come back on the next three runs, so it looks like a one-off stall
  rather than something the furniture does, but I can't say for sure.

  Hits: still about 20 ms each. A hit rebuilds only one or two chunks, so the
  take-it-out-of-the-world trick isn't used there. The first hit on an untouched
  piece costs about the same as later ones.

  The furniture checks still pass: all 150 pieces settle with nothing lost, all
  48 openable pieces open, and doors still follow a carried piece and come off
  when the hinge is smashed. The test scripts are still in tools/_t/.

❯ @"E:\Projects\tm-game\perf_logs\summary.csv"                                  
  Better?                                                                       
  ⎿  Read perf_logs\summary.csv (3 lines)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● Yes, much better, and the room-loading hitches are gone.

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────┬────────┐
  │ High preset (V-Sync off) │ Before  │  Now   │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ Avg FPS                  │ 197     │ 213    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 99% of frames under      │ 11.4 ms │ 9.8 ms │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 1% low                   │ 55 FPS  │ 79 FPS │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ Worst frame              │ 63 ms   │ 53 ms  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ Frames over 33 ms        │ 5       │ 1      │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────────┴────────┘

  The first 21 seconds, while rooms were loading, had no frame over 20 ms at 
  all. That's the part this change targeted. The 1% low going from 55 to 79 FPS
  comes mostly from that.

  The 6 frames over 20 ms all come after 21 s, when you were smashing things.
  The scene's node count climbs steadily from 1,784 to 2,143 then, which is
  rubble and broken pieces piling up. Three of those frames show the physics
  step itself jumping to 20–47 ms; for those, the cause is physics rather than
  graphics. Your one frame over 33 ms (53 ms at 26.9 s) wasn't one of them:
  physics was only 2 ms there, so that one isn't explained by this log. Those
  frames are probably the hits I mentioned last time, which are still about 20
  ms each, combined with the burst of new rubble bodies the engine has to
  settle.

  If you want to smooth out destruction next, there are two options:

  1. Rebuild the hit chunk on a background thread, the way showroom loading now
     works. The chunk keeps its old mesh until the new one is ready, so the hit
     itself stops costing 20 ms on the main thread.
  2. Spread the rubble out over time: cap how many new rubble bodies appear per
     frame and add them over a few frames, so a big smash doesn't hit the
     physics engine all at once.
